Його книжка 1483 року стала першою книжкою українця, виданою за кордоном, і в ній він навів координати Львова та рідного Дрогобича. Навіть далеко від дому він підписувався «магістр Георгіус з Русі». Кожен, хто сьогодні їде вчитися у світ, може взяти з нього приклад: можна стати своїм у великій науці й не забути, звідки ти. |

<details><summary>English, batch 26</summary>

- **Vladyslav Horodetskyi.** Vladyslav Horodetskyi, a Pole from Podillia, lived most of his life in Ukraine and gave Kyiv buildings that still shape the centre of the city. The museum that [Nikola Tereshchenko](/en/people/nikola-tereshchenko/) helped pay for is now the National Art Museum of Ukraine, and his kenesa is the House of Actors. He built for different communities, Karaites and Catholics alike, and each time chose a style of its own: Neo-Greek, Moorish, Neo-Gothic, Art Nouveau. The House with Chimaeras, with its stone elephants and frogs on the roof, shows that architecture can be bold and witty. Anyone on Bankova Street can see how one person can change the look of a city.
- **Volodymyr Borovykovskyi.** Volodymyr Borovykovskyi learned to paint in Myrhorod from his father, an icon painter: together they painted churches, and the young Cossack made portraits of Cossack officers. With that home training he became an academician in Saint Petersburg and one of the empire’s finest portraitists. He dared to paint the empress out for a walk in a park rather than on a throne, and in over 150 portraits blended classicism with sentimentalism. His work hangs in the National Art Museum of Ukraine and one painting is in the Louvre. He left all he owned to the poor. His path shows that a great master can grow out of a father’s workshop.
- **Yurii Drohobych (Kotermak).** Yurii Drohobych showed that a young man from a small Galician town could become a notable figure in the European science of his day. He earned doctorates in Bologna, served as rector of one of Europe’s oldest universities and a professor of medicine in Kraków. His book of 1483 was the first by a Ukrainian published abroad, and in it he gave the coordinates of Lviv and of his home town, Drohobych. Even far from home he signed himself “Master Georgius of Rus’”. Anyone who goes abroad to study today can take him as an example: you can belong in great science and still remember where you come from.

</details>

Навіть поранених російських солдатів вона лікувала, бо це військовополонені. Її вибір показує, що можна лишатися людяною навіть там, де від ворога такого не чекаєш. <details><summary>English, batch 27</summary>

- **Lesya Ukrainka.** Lesya Ukrainka showed that strength of spirit can outweigh physical weakness: she sought treatment for tuberculosis all her life, and all that time she wrote. She wrote about what stirred all of Europe: freedom, dignity, the choice between submission and struggle. The Forest Song, inspired by the legends of Volhynia, is one of the best-known Ukrainian plays, and her poem “Contra spem spero!” became a symbol of resilience for many. She funded an expedition that recorded Ukrainian dumy on a phonograph. Anyone who won’t give up can say with her: “Without hope, I hope!” And the 200-hryvnia banknote carries her lines: “For truth, brothers, let us unite sincerely.”
- **Serhii Korolov.** A Zhytomyr-born engineer who once fell for gliders and rockets led the work that opened the way to space: under his leadership the R-7 rocket was built, which put the first Earth satellite into orbit, then the Vostok spacecraft with Yuri Gagarin. Yet Korolov worked for the state that had thrown him into a Kolyma labor camp without grounds and kept his name secret while he lived. His story is about the talent of a man born in Ukraine who changed human history, and about how a totalitarian system used and silenced its creators. Today that name can, and should, be spoken aloud.
- **Kateryna Bilokur.** Bilokur proved that real art can be born anywhere: in a village, without art training, through talent and persistence. Her parents didn’t want her to paint and the college turned her away, so she learned from nature, mixed her paints from beetroot and viburnum and made her own brushes. Her flowers combine precise observation with the decorative spirit of folk art, and in 1954 three of her canvases were shown at an international exhibition in Paris. As she herself wrote: “So how can I not paint them, when they are so beautiful?” Anyone told they’ll get nowhere without the right certificate can remember her road from Bohdanivka.
- **Ivan Sirko.** Sirko became one of the symbols of Cossack valor and of Ukraine’s defense against raids. Behind the symbol stand real deeds: a single campaign against Kaffa set about 2,000 captives free, and in 1677–1678, together with the forces of Hetman Ivan Samoilovych, he halted the Turkish and Tatar advance. The Zaporozhians elected him kish otaman again and again, even after he had been exiled to Tobolsk. His exploits are celebrated in dumas, and people remembered him above all as the man who freed captives. His example is a reminder that courage is also measured by those it sets free.
- **Yuliia Paievska.** Taira’s footage, which showed the world the siege of Mariupol, was seen by millions of people: a camera meant for a documentary became a record of medics saving the wounded in a besieged city. Since 2014 she had been teaching tactical medicine in Donbas and leading the “Taira’s Angels” paramedic team, so the people she trained know how to save lives too. She treated even wounded Russian soldiers, because they were prisoners of war. Her choice shows that it is possible to stay humane even where the enemy offers no such mercy. For this work she received the US International Women of Courage Award and Ukraine’s Order of Freedom.

</details>

На неспокійному південному кордоні, де постійно доводилося відбивати татарські напади, він спирався на запорозьких козаків, а з укріплення на острові ходив походами на Очаків та Іслам-Кермен. Як пишуть історики, навіть у полоні він відмовився перейти на турецьку службу, і народна дума про козака Байду зберегла образ людини, яка не служить султанові. Тож кожен, хто сьогодні чує про Хортицю й Січ, чує і про початок, закладений ним. |

<details><summary>English, batch 28</summary>

- **Mykhailo Hrushevskyi.** Hrushevskyi gave Ukrainians a scholarly history of their own people: in his ten-volume History of Ukraine-Rus’ he showed that Ukrainian history runs unbroken from Kyivan Rus’, separate from Russia’s. The chair he took over in Lviv became, in effect, the first chair of Ukrainian history, and under his leadership the Shevchenko Scientific Society grew into a true academy of sciences. In 1917 the scholar led the Central Rada from autonomy to the independence of the Ukrainian People’s Republic. What makes a person Ukrainian, he wrote, is their own will and awareness, not origin, faith or rank. The Verkhovna Rada, Ukraine’s parliament, stands today on a street named after him.
- **Pylyp Orlyk.** On the day he became hetman, Pylyp Orlyk signed a document that limited his own power. His Pacts and Constitutions provided for regular General Councils of officers and delegates from the regiments and Zaporizhzhia, and secured the rights of the Cossacks. The Constitution never took effect, but it is an early Ukrainian vision of power limited by law and accountable to a council. In exile, for more than 30 years, he corresponded with European courts and became one of the first Ukrainian émigré politicians to represent the cause of Ukrainian statehood in Europe. Anyone who believes power must answer to the law can count him as a forerunner.
- **Andriy Shevchenko.** Shevchenko proved that a footballer from the Ukrainian school of football could become the best in Europe: the Dynamo Kyiv graduate became the first Ukrainian to win the Champions League and went on to win the Ballon d'Or. He was the first to play 100 games for Ukraine, scored 48 goals for the national team and captained the side that reached the 2006 World Cup quarterfinals; later, as coach, he took the team into the last eight at Euro 2020. After the full-scale invasion he became the first ambassador of UNITED24. His career bears out his own words: “anything is possible if you truly love what you do.”
- **Princess Olha.** Olha is the first known woman ruler of Rus’. Her reforms strengthened the state: instead of poliuddia, the dangerous tribute rounds on which Ihor had been killed, she set up princely pohosty and stations across the country, through which Kyiv collected taxes, held courts and governed its lands. With Byzantium she negotiated rather than fought, and around 959 she sent an embassy to the German king Otto I. Her baptism paved the way for her grandson Volodymyr, who baptized Rus’ in 988. Her example shows that a state is built not only by campaigns but by order and agreements. The Church venerates her as a saint equal to the apostles.
- **Dmytro Vyshnevetskyi (Baida).** Dmytro Vyshnevetskyi is called the first Cossack otaman in the history of Ukraine, and the Khortytsia reserve calls his town and fortress on Mala Khortytsia the first Zaporozhian Sich in history. On a southern frontier under constant Tatar raids he relied on the Zaporozhian Cossacks, and from his island fortress he led campaigns against Ochakiv and Islam-Kermen. Historians write that even in captivity he refused to enter Turkish service, and the folk duma about the Cossack Baida keeps the image of a man who will not serve the sultan. Anyone who hears of Khortytsia and the Sich today is hearing about a beginning he made.

</details>

Його шлях показує кожному юному футболістові, що з київського стадіону можна вийти на вершину європейського футболу. |

<details><summary>English, batch 29</summary>

- **Lina Kostenko.** Lina Kostenko’s life shows that an honest word can be silenced for years but not destroyed. For sixteen years her books did not come out and finished collections were pulled from production, yet she kept writing: Berestechko, written mostly in the late 1960s, was published only in 1999. She did not stay silent when Ukrainian intellectuals were put on trial, and she signed open letters against the arrests. Her lines from Berestechko, “For only nations revealed in the Word can live with dignity on earth,” remind us that language and literature are part of a people’s freedom. Anyone told to keep quiet can lean on her example.
- **Hryhorii Skovoroda.** Skovoroda chose freedom over a career: when the bishop pressed him to become a monk, he gave up teaching and spent a quarter-century with no property or post, living by his own views. His idea of “kindred work”, work you have a natural calling for, speaks to anyone choosing a profession. He taught that happiness lies not in wealth and fame but in knowing yourself and living simply and honestly. The words he asked for on his grave, “The world tried to catch me, but did not catch me,” still encourage anyone who won’t live by others’ measure. His portrait is on the 500-hryvnia banknote.
- **Andrei Sheptytskyi.** Sheptytskyi showed how a church can stand by its people, by education and by humanity, even in the darkest times. For 43 years he led the church under four regimes and looked beyond faith: he founded the National Museum in Lviv and a hospital, and supported schools. Under Nazi occupation he protested to Himmler against the killing of Jews and condemned it in his pastoral letter “Thou Shalt Not Kill”. Together with his brother Klymentii he organized the hiding of Jews, mostly children, in church monasteries; an estimated 150 to 200 were saved. His words “The world is perishing for lack of love, perishing from human hatred!” still speak today.
- **Les Kurbas.** Kurbas proved that Ukrainian theater could be intellectual and innovative, the equal of the best European stages. He travelled to Warsaw, Prague, Berlin and Munich to see the best and brought it home: he gave the first Ukrainian stagings of Oedipus Rex and Macbeth and opened Ukrainian theater to European modernism. With Mykola Kulish and Vadym Meller he created the Berezil’s finest productions, and he trained a generation of actors and directors, among them Amvrosii Buchma and Nataliia Uzhvii. Even in the camp he kept staging plays. A State Prize bears his name, and anyone who boldly renews Ukrainian art carries on his work.
- **Oleh Blokhin.** Blokhin was the first Soviet winner of the Ballon d'Or and set records that no one in Soviet football ever surpassed. A product of the Dynamo Kyiv football school, he played for his home club for almost twenty years, won the Cup Winners’ Cup with it twice, and in 1975 beat Bayern Munich twice. As a coach he led the team of independent Ukraine to its first World Cup, where it reached the quarterfinals. He himself says he is proud to represent “the Ukrainian school of football”. His path shows every young player that a Kyiv kid can reach the top of European football.

</details>

<details><summary>English, batch 30</summary>

- **Mykola Lysenko.** Lysenko proved that Ukrainian music could be classical music. All his life he wrote down folk songs and kobzar melodies, and from them grew his operas, choral works, art songs and over 80 settings of poems from Kobzar: “Folklore is life itself.” He refused to have Taras Bulba staged in Russian, as he accepted only Ukrainian for his operas, even when it was banned from the words to sheet music. His school in Kyiv was the first in eastern Ukraine to teach music and drama in Ukrainian. And his choral piece “O God the Great, the Only One” (“Prayer for Ukraine”) became the country’s spiritual anthem, sung on state holidays.
- **Mykola Vinhranovskyi.** Vinhranovskyi is considered one of the brightest innovators of Ukrainian verse in the 1960s to 1980s. When he was expected to repent of “formalism”, he did not; he read a poem in reply: “If my people are counted formally, / Then I am truly a true formalist!” A student of [Oleksandr Dovzhenko](/en/people/oleksandr-dovzhenko/), he became a director himself and made documentaries about Chyhyryn, Baturyn and Halych. His poems and stories for children, such as Siromanets, speak just as clearly to young readers as to adults. He showed that even under pressure you can stay yourself and simply get on with your work: writing and filming.
- **Mykola Zerov.** Zerov and the Neoclassicists proved that Ukrainian poetry could be part of the great European tradition, from Rome to Ronsard’s France. His Anthology of Roman Poetry and Kamena opened Catullus, Virgil, Horace and Ovid to Ukrainian readers. With his slogan “Ad fontes!” (“To the sources!”) he called for learning from cultured Europe rather than settling for “proletarian hackwork”. He wrote polished sonnets himself and, as a professor, headed a department of translation theory, so he taught both craft and translation. Anyone today who translates world classics into Ukrainian, or writes with care for form, follows his path.
- **Taras Shevchenko.** Shevchenko proved that great literature could be written in Ukrainian: modern Ukrainian literature began with his Kobzar. He put into words the ideas Ukrainian national identity grew from: freedom, dignity and resistance to oppression. His own life ran from a serf whose freedom artists bought to an academician of engraving, and even exile with orders not to write or draw did not break him. His “Testament” has been translated into more than 60 languages, and his words “Fight on, and you will prevail!” still ring out today for everyone defending their freedom.
- **Ostap Vyshnia.** Ostap Vyshnia is a classic of Ukrainian humor. In the 1920s only Shevchenko’s works outsold his books in total print run, and his “smiles” talked with readers about the village, language and Ukrainization, and mocked Russian great-power chauvinism. His fate shows how the Soviet regime broke artists: one of the most popular writers of his day spent ten years in camps and still went back to writing humor. It was then that he finished Hunting Smiles, about forests, rivers and people out in nature. His work still shows that laughter in Ukrainian can be both comfort and sharp satire.

</details>

<details><summary>English, batch 31</summary>

- **Kvitka Cisyk.** Kvitka Cisyk could have remained just a voice from American commercials: from the early 1980s she was one of the most popular jingle singers in the United States. But this New Yorker, the daughter of a family from Lviv, never let go of what Saturday school of Ukrainian studies and Plast camps had given her. She paid for two albums of Ukrainian songs herself, Kvitka: Songs of Ukraine and Two Colors, and brought many listeners to Ukrainian song; the albums are still heard in Ukraine. She wanted to weave “joyful notes” into the fabric of her people’s life, and she showed that a culture can be cherished far from home.
- **Volodymyr Vernadskyi.** Vernadskyi was one of the first to see that life is a geological force that changes a whole planet. He stood at the start of new sciences, geochemistry and biogeochemistry, and his theory of the biosphere and the noosphere influenced modern ecological thinking. He did as much for Ukraine: in 1918 he led the creation of the Ukrainian Academy of Sciences, became its first president and helped found the National Library of Ukraine, which now bears his name. His idea of the noosphere, in which human reason becomes the main force changing the planet, is summed up in his own words about the future: “It is in our hands.”
- **Mariana Mamonova.** Mariana’s story showed the world that Russia holds medics, women and even mothers-to-be as prisoners. In besieged Mariupol, after learning she was pregnant, she stayed with her battalion and kept treating the wounded. She turned what she had been through into help for others: the charity she founded in 2024 helps women who survived Russian captivity get back on their feet, and her “baby boxes” go to newborns in soldiers’ families. Her path shows that an ordeal survived can become support for those who come after.
- **Mustafa Dzhemilev.** For many Crimean Tatars and Ukrainians, Dzhemilev is a symbol of resistance to deportation and occupation. From 1961 he fought for his people’s right to return to Crimea, spent 15 years in captivity for it and did not back down. He co-founded the Union of Crimean Tatar Youth, led the Mejlis for 22 years, and defended Crimean Tatar rights in parliament and as the President’s Commissioner. His life shows that a people’s rights can be defended for decades, even behind bars: “freedom cannot be traded for any material goods.” It is also a reminder that Crimea is home to the Crimean Tatars, driven out of it more than once.
- **Marko Vovchok.** Marko Vovchok was one of the first to show serfdom through a woman’s eyes: her Folk Stories, told in the voices of peasant serf women, were at once a landmark in Ukrainian literature, and [Shevchenko](/en/people/taras-shevchenko/) called her his “daughter”. She opened new genres in Ukrainian literature, including prose for children, and her translations of Jules Verne and Darwin opened world literature and science to readers. Her story shows how someone who grew up outside the Ukrainian-speaking world could consciously choose Ukrainian as the language of her work. She proved that the voices of people long unheard can become great literature.

</details>

Він залишив тисячі творів - від «Каменярів» і «Захара Беркута», повісті про громаду, яка обстоює свою волю, до «Мойсея», роздуму про народ, лідера й шлях до свободи. Він перекладав з 14 мов, досліджував фольклор та історію літератури і став одним із засновників першої української політичної партії. А 1900 року, у статті «Поза межами можливого», одним з перших обґрунтував ідею політично незалежної України. Його «Лиш боротись значить жить» і сьогодні звучить як порада не здаватися. |

<details><summary>English, batch 32</summary>

- **Kazimir Malevich.** Malevich showed that a painting need not depict anything at all: it can be pure form and color. His Black Square became a symbol of 20th-century abstract art, and his ideas still shape painting, design and architecture. He did more than paint: he explained the new art in books and articles and taught students, El Lissitzky among them. Born in Kyiv, he studied at Mykola Murashko’s drawing school there and later came back to teach and exhibit. His path shows that one bold idea can change the art of the whole world.
- **Anna of Kyiv.** Anna’s story shows that Kyivan Rus’ was part of the political life of medieval Europe. A daughter of the Prince of Kyiv became Queen of France and had a say in affairs of state: twenty-eight documents issued in her name or with her participation show that she regularly sat in on the royal council. She raised a son who became King Philip I, and she founded (or restored) the Monastery of Saint Vincent in Senlis. That is why she stands today as a symbol of Ukraine’s old ties with Europe, and her first monument went up in 2005 in Senlis itself.
- **Mykola Amosov.** Amosov showed that science, hard work and discipline save lives. He was the first in Ukraine to operate on the heart with artificial circulation, the first in the USSR to replace a mitral valve with a prosthesis, and the first in the world to create heart valve prostheses that prevent blood clots. His clinic grew into an institute that in time performed about 3,000 heart operations a year. And through his books for general readers and his “limits and loads” system, he taught people to look after their own health. His example shows that the courage to do what no one has done before turns into lives saved.
- **Borys Paton.** Technologies from Paton’s school are used in building pipelines and bridges, in rocket engineering and in medicine. With H. Voloshkevych he created electroslag welding for powerful power plants and heavy equipment. The institute he led from 1953 to 2020 carried out the world’s first welding experiments in space, and under his direction it developed a way of welding living tissue that surgeons now use. He headed the Academy of Sciences for 58 years and, after 1991, despite sharp cuts in funding, is considered to have preserved its main scientific schools. His life shows that science made in Kyiv can work in a factory, in space and in an operating room.
- **Ivan Franko.** Franko brought literature, scholarship and public life together and became one of the most influential Ukrainian intellectuals of his time. He left thousands of works, from “The Stonebreakers” and Zakhar Berkut, a novel about a community standing up for its freedom, to Moses, a reflection on a people, a leader and the road to freedom. He translated from 14 languages, studied folklore and literary history, and co-founded the first Ukrainian political party. And in 1900, in “Beyond the Limits of the Possible”, he was one of the first to argue for a politically independent Ukraine. “Only to struggle is to live” still reads as advice not to give up.

</details>

<details><summary>English, batch 33</summary>

- **Sergey Bubka.** Bubka is called one of the greatest athletes of the 20th century. Until 1985 no pole vaulter had cleared 6 metres; he did it first and set world records 35 times. Both his records stood for more than 20 years, and no other athlete has won six world titles in the same individual event. The boy who started vaulting in Luhansk showed that one person can set the bar for the world for years. His 6 metres became a new horizon for pole vaulters, and his six world titles in a row a yardstick for a long career at the top.
- **Olena Stepaniv.** Olena Stepaniv disguised herself as a man to join the Sich Riflemen Legion, and her bravery at Makivka earned her officer rank, which very few women held in any army of the First World War. She fought for a Ukrainian state and helped prepare the November uprising; after the war she helped build Ukrainian geography: a Vienna doctorate, over 75 scholarly works, teaching in Lviv. Banned from the classroom, she went on working for Ridna Shkola. She paid for it with ten years of unfreedom. Her story is a reminder that the “firsts” of Ukrainian history often earned that word not through one feat, but through an entire life.
- **Nikolai Gogol.** Gogol showed the world a vivid picture of Ukraine: its people, customs, history and humor. The son of Ukrainian nobles from the Poltava region, whose father wrote comedies in Ukrainian, he made his name with stories of the Ukrainian village, full of humor, folklore and the supernatural. Evenings on a Farm Near Dikanka, Taras Bulba and Viy brought Ukraine into world literature, while The Government Inspector, Dead Souls and The Overcoat shaped the development of world prose. After critics tore apart his first poem, he found his own voice in what he had known since childhood, an example for anyone who writes about home.
- **Yaroslav the Wise.** Yaroslav showed that a state’s strength lies not only in its army but in its laws, learning and culture. The Rus’ Justice is the first known written law code of Rus’, and its oldest part is tied to his name. At Saint Sophia he founded a school and a library, where scribes translated and copied books. Saint Sophia and the Golden Gate made Kyiv an architectural rival of Constantinople, and his daughters’ marriages linked Rus’ with France, Norway and Hungary. He won Kyiv in a long struggle, yet he is remembered for what he built. Little wonder his portrait is on today’s 2-hryvnia banknote.
- **Mykola Leontovych.** A village singing teacher from Podillia who spent years polishing each arrangement created a melody the world now sings. “Shchedryk” is an example of cultural diplomacy: the Ukrainian Republic Capella, set up with the backing of the Ukrainian People’s Republic, carried it across Europe and the Americas, as far as Carnegie Hall, and a Ukrainian New Year’s song became part of Christmas worldwide. It has since been rearranged more than 150 times. Whenever “Carol of the Bells” plays, anyone can say it began as a Ukrainian song about a swallow. At the same time, Leontovych’s fate is a reminder of how many Ukrainian artists the Soviet regime destroyed.

</details>

<details><summary>English, batch 34</summary>

- **Vasyl Symonenko.** Vasyl Symonenko lived only 28 years and published a single collection, yet his poems reached people past the censors, retyped and passed from hand to hand. He wrote about the hard life of the peasants, mocked the Soviet system, called for resisting despotism and great-power chauvinism, and wrote of his love for Ukraine. Those poems largely mark the beginning of the Ukrainian opposition movement of the 1960s and 1970s, and Symonenko became one of the defining figures in the struggle for Ukraine’s state and cultural sovereignty. His lines “You know that you are a human being. / Do you know it or not?” still speak to every reader.
- **Serge Lifar.** Lifar changed 20th-century European ballet. A boy from outside Kyiv who only took up dance in 1920 was a soloist with Diaghilev three years later and went on to spend almost thirty years at the Paris Opera. He created more than 200 ballets and dance pieces, among them On the Dnipro, and is regarded as the founder of the neoclassical style in choreography. He passed on what he knew, founding the Opera’s Institute of Choreography and teaching dance history and theory at the Sorbonne. His path shows that a late start can still lead to a great stage. Since 1994 Kyiv has hosted the Serge Lifar International Ballet Competition.
- **Danylo of Halych.** After the fall of Kyiv, Danylo kept a Rus’ state alive in the west and tied it to Europe. A child refugee who had lost his father’s state, he spent thirty years winning it back: he united Volhynia, took Halych with the townspeople’s support and beat his rivals at Yaroslav. Between the Horde and the West he looked for ways to keep his state safe: he built fortresses, created an infantry of peasants and in 1253 became the first king of Rus’. And Lviv, founded in his time, became one of Ukraine’s main cities. His story shows that what is lost can be won back with patience and work.
- **Mykola Khvylovy.** Mykola Khvylovy put a question to Ukrainian culture that stirred up the whole literary world: should it look to Moscow or to a “psychological Europe”? Young writers took up his bold style, and VAPLITE formed around him. He wrote that Ukrainian art “must step out onto the great international stage” rather than be “a provincial corner of Russian culture.” Right after his death his works, and even his name, were banned, yet today Khvylovy is a symbol of the Executed Renaissance, the generation of artists of the 1920s–1930s destroyed by the Soviet authorities. And his idea of a European path for Ukraine sounds relevant again.
- **Vira Kholodna.** Vira Kholodna was one of the first film stars in the modern sense: audiences called her the “queen of the screen,” though her whole career lasted less than five years. Born in Poltava, she became a star after a single film, made dozens more, and in Odesa, where her troupe moved, also performed at charity concerts. She was born and died in Ukraine, although she worked mostly in Moscow cinema. Only a few of her films survive, among them Children of the Age and Mirages, and they let us see the actress herself behind the legends.

</details>

<details><summary>English, batch 35</summary>

- **Leonid Kadeniuk.** Kadeniuk went from a village boy who heard about Gagarin’s flight at the age of ten to a test pilot picked from 9,000 candidates. His flight on Columbia showed that independent Ukraine could be an equal partner in space research: on board he ran a joint Ukrainian-American experiment with plants in weightlessness. He took Shevchenko’s Kobzar and the national flag with him, and thanks to him the Ukrainian flag was unfurled in space for the first time. For many people he became proof that a childhood dream of space can come true.
- **Levko Lukianenko.** Lukianenko defended Ukraine’s right to leave the USSR at a time when it could get him shot. His underground union looked for a lawful way to do it, a referendum based on the Soviet constitutions, and for that he spent 72 days waiting to be executed. Released after his first term, he co-founded the Ukrainian Helsinki Group that same year and was soon behind bars again. Three decades after his death sentence, he was the one who wrote the document with which independent Ukraine began. His story shows that an idea once punished as “treason” can become the foundation of a state.
- **Yaryna Chornohuz.** Yaryna Chornohuz writes about the war from the inside, as someone who saves the wounded under fire. Her poems, written “on the out-breath after a completed mission,” won Ukraine’s highest award for the arts, and the government described their author simply as a member of the Armed Forces. She dedicated her first collection, “How the War Circle Bends”, to Mykola Sorochuk. “[dasein]” was translated into French, and in 2026 it won the Alain Bosquet Prize in Paris, carrying a Ukrainian soldier’s voice far beyond the front line. Her path shows that service and writing need not exclude each other: one can defend a country and give it new poetry.
- **Viacheslav Chornovil.** Chornovil showed that words and documents can stand up to a totalitarian system. His The Misfortune of Intellect, published abroad, told of the closed trials of Ukrainian intellectuals. More than 13 years in captivity did not silence him: even in the camps he kept writing appeals. Back home, he was among the initiators of the Act of Declaration of Independence, and later turned down a place in government to stay in opposition. His slogan “Ukraine begins with you” reads as a call to personal responsibility. And about his own life he said that if he had to start all over again, “I would choose the life I have lived.”
- **Liubomyr Huzar.** Huzar brought the UGCC’s seat back to Kyiv and proposed a path towards unity among the Ukrainian churches of the Kyivan tradition. He had been made a bishop in secret, in case the Soviet authorities destroyed the underground Church; instead he returned to Ukraine and came to lead it. He stressed the Church’s social teaching, backed civil society and sought dialogue with the Orthodox churches. After retiring, as he lost his sight, he co-founded the First of December Initiative Group. His words from the Euromaidan, “Let us remember who we are and not let ourselves be fooled”, still hold. In 2024 the process for his beatification began.

</details>

<details><summary>English, batch 36</summary>

- **Bohdan Stupka.** Stupka became a symbol of Ukrainian theater at the turn of the 21st century. He acted in plays by Ivan Franko and [Lesya Ukrainka](/en/people/lesya-ukrainka/) and in The Aeneid after [Ivan Kotliarevskyi](/en/people/ivan-kotliarevskyi/), and his Tevye, after [Sholem Aleichem](/en/people/sholom-aleikhem/), won the Shevchenko Prize: on his stage Ukrainian and world classics stood side by side. Krzysztof Zanussi, Jerzy Hoffman, Régis Wargnier and Otar Iosseliani worked with him too. For more than a decade he led the Ivan Franko Theater, and served as Minister of Culture. He dreamed of Ukrainian culture becoming “known all over the world” and was happy to help make it happen. His roles remain a benchmark of the actor’s craft.
- **Roksolana.** Roksolana’s fate is a reminder of the Ukrainians who in those times were taken captive and sold into slavery. A girl from Rus’ who entered the harem as a slave became the sultan’s lawful wife, the first “haseki” and the mother of Sultan Selim II. She founded mosques, a women’s hospital, schools and soup kitchens for the poor, from Istanbul to Mecca and Medina. Yet little about her is certain: even the name “Roksolana” was invented by a foreign ambassador, and the image of a patriot came from later writers. Her story also teaches us to tell facts from legends.
- **Ruslana Lyzhychko.** Ruslana’s victory showed that the sounds of the Carpathians could feel modern on Europe’s biggest stage. A trembita, Hutsul culture and Ukrainian sung alongside English brought Ukraine its first Eurovision win. Even earlier she had paired medieval ballads with modern music and toured Ukraine’s castles to draw attention to their restoration. On the Maidan she used her fame to support the protesters and draw the world’s attention to Ukraine, singing the anthem from the stage more than 500 times. Her path shows that tradition can sound new, and that fame can serve one’s country.
- **Ahatanhel Krymskyi.** Krymskyi showed that Ukrainian scholarship could be world-class and still speak Ukrainian. He laid the foundations of Oriental studies in Ukraine and translated from Arabic, Persian and Turkish. He did so much for the young Academy of Sciences that its staff jokingly called it the “Krymskyi Academy”; he supervised the rules of Ukrainian orthography and refuted the claim that the language of ancient Kyiv was not Ukrainian. A man of Crimean Tatar and Belarusian roots, he called himself a conscious Ukrainian. The Institute of Oriental Studies of the National Academy of Sciences of Ukraine bears his name.
- **Andriana Susak-Arekhta.** Andriana Susak-Arekhta fought at a time when women were not even allowed to hold combat posts officially. “Invisible Battalion”, the film in which she is one of the heroines, campaigned for that to change, and in 2018 women were allowed into combat roles. The Women Veterans Movement she helped found stands up for servicewomen and veterans and plants Alleys of Memory for fallen women defenders. Her story shows that women fight alongside men, and that this should show not only on the battlefield but on paper too. Every woman serving today can build on what she and other veterans achieved.

</details>

<details><summary>English, batch 37</summary>

- **Anton Makarenko.** After years of war and revolution, Anton Makarenko took on the children no school wanted: homeless kids and young offenders. In his colony they lived in brigades and helped run the place themselves, and his commune near Kharkiv became home to many children left homeless by the Holodomor. A Pedagogical Poem, his account of that work, was translated into 58 languages, and educators in many countries still discuss his ideas about children’s self-government and learning through work. His story also shows how a gifted teacher from the Sumy region ended up serving the Soviet system.
- **Averkii Honcharenko.** At Kruty, Averkii Honcharenko commanded cadets and students, among them young men from the school he had Ukrainized himself. The battle held up the Bolshevik advance on Kyiv, and it is considered to have given the government time to sign the Treaty of Brest-Litovsk. Honcharenko managed to lead the rest of his force out in good order, and later wrote one of the first detailed memoirs of the battle. In exile he helped disabled Ukrainian soldiers and veterans. Kruty became a symbol of young people giving their lives for their country, and Honcharenko’s story is a reminder that behind the symbol stand real people with complicated lives.
- **Borys Baranov.** The Chornobyl disaster could have been even worse. Water still lay under the wrecked reactor, and if the molten fuel reached it, a second explosion could follow. Borys Baranov, with Oleksii Ananenko and Valerii Bespalov, went down into the flooded space and opened the valves by hand to drain it. A legend grew around them, but the truth is as striking: all three survived, and Baranov worked at the plant for another 19 years. His story is a reminder that the disaster was held back not only by firefighters and soldiers, but also by plant workers who knew their equipment well and went where they were needed.
- **Danylo Zabolotnyi.** Danylo Zabolotnyi put science to the test on himself: he drank a live cholera culture to make sure a vaccine really protected. He was among the first to show that healthy people can carry cholera, and in 1911 he proved that plague passes to people from wild rodents. These findings helped show how to stop epidemics. In Odesa he founded the world’s first department of epidemiology, and in Kyiv an institute that bears his name today. A boy from a peasant family in Podillia became president of the All-Ukrainian Academy of Sciences, and showed that science takes courage as well as knowledge.
- **Denys Prokopenko.** Denys Prokopenko never trained as a soldier: his degree is in teaching English, and he joined Azov in 2014. He led the defence of encircled Mariupol for over 80 days. Awarding the Azovstal commanders the Gold Star, the head of the Office of the President said they and their troops “managed to tie down huge enemy forces. Forces that the enemy lacked to occupy larger territories.” Prokopenko was among the last to leave Azovstal on orders, survived captivity and took command of Azov again. In 2024 he wrote of captive Azov fighters: “We will bring everyone back.” As of October 2026 Brigadier General Prokopenko commands the Azov grouping of forces.

</details>

Його київські праці - «Володимир», поетика й риторика - залишаються частиною історії української літератури. А «Духовний регламент», який він написав для Петра I, майже 200 років підпорядковував церкву імперській державі. |

Історія Гордійчука - про те, що тяжке ранення не завершує службу: він повернувся до строю як вихователь нового покоління офіцерів. | **Чому це важливо сьогодні**<br>Савур-Могила стала символом того, що невелика група може тримати ключову позицію проти переважаючих сил: 14 розвідників Гордійчука 12 днів утримували висоту й коригували вогонь артилерії. Після тяжкого поранення він вижив і не залишив служби. Ще не завершивши реабілітацію, він очолив Київський військовий ліцей імені [Івана Богуна](/uk/people/ivan-bohun/) і сім років виховував підлітків, які після 2022 року самі пішли захищати країну. У листопаді 2023 року він перейшов до Національного університету оборони України. Історія Гордійчука - про те, що тяжке ранення не завершує службу. |

<details><summary>English, batch 38</summary>

- **Dmytro Kliachkivskyi.** Dmytro Kliachkivskyi was one of the organizers of the UPA and is credited with building up its structure, tactics and discipline. At the same time, historians link the mass killings of Polish civilians in Volhynia in 1943 to decisions of the leadership he headed there, and some OUN leaders did not share that policy. Kliachkivskyi’s story is a reminder that the fight for one’s own state and crimes against neighbours can be tied to the same people. An honest conversation about 1943, the shared work of Ukrainian and Polish historians drawing on testimony and reports, matters for understanding between the two nations.
- **Dmytro Kozatskyi.** Because of Kozatskyi’s photos, the defence of Azovstal has faces: not numbers in a report, but real people, wounded and alive, in light and in darkness. He photographed his comrades in the tunnels of the besieged plant and managed to share the pictures with the world before going into captivity. While he was a prisoner, his series “The Light Will Win” was shown in Ukraine and abroad and won a photography contest in Paris. His pictures still tell the world about Mariupol, and show that even under siege the truth can be kept and passed on.
- **Elina Svitolina.** Before Elina Svitolina, Ukraine had no Olympic medal in tennis, and no Ukrainian woman had 20 WTA titles. She won the WTA Finals and reached world No. 3, and after a break to become a mother she won a tournament within four months of her return and made the Wimbledon semifinals again that summer. Her Tokyo bronze and 20 titles showed that a Ukrainian player can stay among the world’s best for years. Her foundation develops tennis in Ukraine and supports young athletes, and since 2022, as an ambassador of UNITED24 and Bring Kids Back UA, she has helped keep the world from forgetting Ukraine during the war.
- **Feofan Prokopovych.** Prokopovych is one of the Kyiv-Mohyla graduates who shaped the church, education and government of the whole Russian Empire. In Kyiv he defended the theories of Copernicus, Galileo and Kepler, introduced the teaching of mathematics and geometry, and introduced students to the ideas of Descartes, Bacon and Locke. His Kyiv works, Vladimir and his books on poetics and rhetoric, remain part of the history of Ukrainian literature. And the Spiritual Regulation he wrote for Peter I kept the church subordinate to the imperial state for almost 200 years.
- **Ihor Hordiichuk.** Savur-Mohyla became a symbol of how a small group can hold a key position against far larger forces: Hordiichuk’s 14 scouts held the hill for 12 days and directed artillery fire. He survived a severe wound and did not leave the service. Before his rehabilitation was even finished, he took charge of the Kyiv Military Lyceum named after [Ivan Bohun](/en/people/ivan-bohun/), and for seven years he raised teenagers who, after 2022, went on to defend the country themselves. In November 2023 he moved to the National Defence University of Ukraine. Hordiichuk’s story shows that a severe wound doesn’t end a life of service.

</details>

Коли його вірші перестали друкувати, він писав для кіно, і «Криницю для спраглих» назвали однією з вершин українського поетичного кіно. 1986 року він першим на з'їзді письменників відкрито заговорив про Голодомор, а 1989-го став першим головою Народного руху України. А історія Дзюби показує, що одна людина зі словом може кинути виклик системі. | Лишається після «Дискусії та оцінки»:<br>**Чому це важливо сьогодні**<br>Іван Дзюба вголос сказав те, що влада хотіла приховати. На прем'єрі «Тіней забутих предків» він заговорив про таємні арешти молодої інтелігенції, і це був один із перших публічних протестів проти політичних репресій у СРСР. У Бабиному Яру він виступив проти антисемітизму. Його праця «Інтернаціоналізм чи русифікація?» розійшлася в самвидаві, вийшла на Заході кількома мовами й досі залишається одним з найважливіших текстів про русифікацію України. У незалежній Україні він був міністром культури й співголовою редколегії Енциклопедії сучасної України. Його історія показує, що одна людина зі словом може кинути виклик системі. |

Його портрет є на банкноті 10 гривень. | **Чому це важливо сьогодні**<br>Іван Мазепа залишив Україні те, що видно й сьогодні: його коштом збудували й відновили багато храмів, зокрема в Києво-Печерській лаврі та Михайлівському Золотоверхому монастирі, а їхній стиль назвали козацьким, або мазепинським, бароко. Він підтримував друкарство, школи й Києво-Могилянську академію. 1708 року, коли цар обмежував автономію Гетьманщини, гетьман перейшов на бік Карла XII і став символом спроби України вийти з-під влади Москви. Його заклик, щоб усі «як один» стали «на захист наших законів і свобод», звучить і сьогодні. Його портрет є на банкноті 10 гривень. |

<details><summary>English, batch 39</summary>

- **Illia Samoilenko.** Illia Samoilenko kept coming back. After losing his left hand and right eye in Donbas, he was fitted with a titanium hook and went back to his comrades in Azov. From besieged Azovstal he and [Sviatoslav Palamar](/en/people/sviatoslav-palamar/) told foreign journalists about the siege and the hundreds of wounded who needed to be evacuated, so the world heard Mariupol’s defenders in their own words. After 120 days in solitary confinement he returned to service once more: as of July 2025 he was in charge of intelligence in Azov. His story shows that even after the heaviest losses, a person can return to the work they believe is theirs.
- **Ivan Bohun.** Ivan Bohun showed what to do when all seems lost: at Berestechko, after the khan betrayed the Cossacks and captured [Bohdan Khmelnytskyi](/en/people/bohdan-khmelnytskyi/), the encircled army chose him as acting hetman, and he led most of it out. He beat Polish forces at Vinnytsia and Monastyryshche, and after the Pereiaslav Council he refused to swear allegiance to the Muscovite tsar. Nor would he accept [Ivan Vyhovskyi](/en/people/ivan-vyhovskyi/)’s pro-Polish policy. Bohun stood up for Ukraine’s self-rule against both Poland and Moscow, and people kept his memory alive in the historical duma “Ivan Bohun.” Anyone who stands up today for Ukraine’s right to decide for itself has a forerunner in him.
- **Ivan Drach.** Ivan Drach showed that Ukrainian poetry could speak in a new way: A Knife in the Sun shocked some readers and struck others as a revelation, and Sunflower, with the debuts of [Mykola Vinhranovskyi](/en/people/mykola-vinhranovskyi/) and [Vasyl Symonenko](/en/people/vasyl-symonenko/), became one of the central literary events of those years. Shut out of the papers, he wrote for the screen: A Well for the Thirsty became a high point of Ukrainian poetic cinema. In 1986 he was the first at the Writers’ Congress to speak openly about the Holodomor, and in 1989 he became the first leader of the People’s Movement of Ukraine. His works have been translated into dozens of languages.
- **Ivan Dziuba.** Ivan Dziuba said out loud what the authorities wanted kept secret. At the premiere of Shadows of Forgotten Ancestors he spoke about the secret arrests of young intellectuals, in one of the first public protests against political repression in the USSR. At Babyn Yar he spoke out against antisemitism. His Internationalism or Russification? spread through samvydav, came out in the West in several languages and remains one of the most important texts on the Russification of Ukraine. In independent Ukraine he served as minister of culture and co-chaired the editorial board of the Encyclopedia of Modern Ukraine. His story shows that one person armed with words can challenge a system.
- **Ivan Mazepa.** Ivan Mazepa left Ukraine something you can still see: his money built and restored many churches, including in the Kyiv Pechersk Lavra and St. Michael’s Golden-Domed Monastery, and their style became known as Cossack, or Mazepa, Baroque. He supported printing, schools and the Kyiv-Mohyla Academy. In 1708, as the tsar kept cutting back the Hetmanate’s autonomy, he went over to Charles XII and became a symbol of Ukraine’s attempt to break free from Moscow’s rule. His call for all to stand “as one” “in defence of our laws and freedoms” still rings true today. His portrait is on the 10-hryvnia banknote.

</details>

<details><summary>English, batch 40</summary>

- **Ivan Piddubnyi.** Ivan Piddubnyi is one of the iconic figures in the history of physical culture. His path from port loader to “Champion of Champions” shows what strength, training and fair competition can achieve: he won by the “Hamburg score” too, and in his fifties he quickly mastered freestyle wrestling in the United States. In 1937 he crossed out “Poddubny” and “Russian” in his own passport and wrote “Piddubnyi” and “Ukrainian”. Neither prison nor torture made him renounce who he was: he defended his right to call himself Ukrainian. Anyone who is told today who they must be can remember his words: “I am a Ukrainian Cossack, and that says it all.”
- **Ivan Puliui.** Ivan Puliui left his mark both on science and on his own people’s culture. His lamp is seen as a forerunner of the X-ray tube, and he was one of the first researchers of X-rays, which no hospital today can do without. In Prague he became the first dean of Europe’s first faculty of electrical engineering and built an alternating-current power station. With Panteleimon Kulish and Ivan Nechui-Levytskyi he translated the Bible into Ukrainian, helping Ukrainians read it in their own language, and he set up a fund for poor students from Ukraine. His life shows that serious science and loyalty to one’s people can go hand in hand.
- **Ivan Vyhovskyi.** Ivan Vyhovskyi rose from a captured cavalry captain in the Polish army to [Bohdan Khmelnytskyi](/en/people/bohdan-khmelnytskyi/)’s general chancellor, in effect the head of the Cossack government. As hetman he sought a place for Cossack Ukraine between Warsaw and Moscow: under the Treaty of Hadiach it would become an autonomous Grand Duchy of Rus’. His name is tied above all to Konotop, where in 1659 the Cossack army, together with the Crimean Tatars, crushed the tsar’s forces. Later, it is thought, he moved on from autonomy to the idea of an independent Ukraine. He is called the first hetman executed for his part in the struggle for the Cossack state’s independence.
- **Khrystyna Sushko.** A woman officer in the Ukrainian army surprises no one today. Khrystyna Sushko showed it was possible more than a hundred years ago: she treated the Sich Riflemen on the battlefield, escaped Bolshevik captivity and told Ukrainian units where the enemy stood, and stayed in service after severe wounds. She is called the first, and perhaps the only, woman officer of the UNR Army. Abroad, too, she cared for her own: she helped wounded comrades in the internment camp, and after the Second World War, as a doctor, looked after displaced persons camps full mostly of Ukrainians. Her path is an example for anyone who serves today to protect others.
- **Leonid Kravchuk.** Leonid Kravchuk was a man of the Soviet system who, at the decisive moment, sided with independence. In August 1991 he refused General Varennikov’s demand for a state of emergency, and on 24 August he chaired the session where 346 deputies voted for the Act of Declaration of Independence. On 1 December he was elected Ukraine’s first president, and a week later he signed the agreement that put an end to the USSR. He said that “my signature is on every document of independent Ukraine”. His story shows how complicated the road from Soviet Ukraine to Ukraine was, and that one can choose one’s own state wherever one starts.

</details>

Його рішення показало, що для захисника головне - побратими, які ще потребують допомоги, і що довіру людей можна передати далі. |

<details><summary>English, batch 41</summary>

- **Lilia Podkopayeva.** Lilia Podkopayeva was one of the first world stars of independent Ukraine’s sport. Months before Atlanta she was badly hurt, yet she came back and, at 17, won the all-around, the biggest title in women’s gymnastics. The hard skills she was first to perform carry her name in the international rules used to judge gymnasts all over the world. After retiring she stayed in the sport as a judge and coach and set up her own tournament in Kyiv. Thirty years after Atlanta she said: “This victory was never mine alone. It has always belonged, and always will belong, to Ukraine.”
- **Marta Kostyuk.** Marta Kostyuk’s story shows that junior success can grow into a senior career. At 15, ranked No. 521, she came through qualifying at the Australian Open and won matches in the main draw. Eight years later she won the WTA 1000 title in Madrid, then reached two Grand Slam semifinals in a row that summer, equalling the best result by a Ukrainian woman at Wimbledon. After the Madrid final against a Russian player there was no handshake, and Kostyuk ended her speech with “glory to God and glory to Ukraine.” Anyone starting out in qualifying can see in her path how far you can go if you keep going.
- **Mustafa Nayyem.** Nayyem’s story shows how one journalist’s call can bring people out onto a square: the rally of 21 November 2013 grew into the Euromaidan, and the Euromaidan into the Revolution of Dignity. That same year he was one of the initiators of Hromadske TV, which went on air in the first days of the protest. Later Nayyem moved from journalism into public service: he helped oversee the launch of the new patrol police, and during the full-scale war he was in charge of roads, border infrastructure and rebuilding. The boy who moved to Ukraine from Kabul at ten showed that one voice can start great change.
- **Mykhail Semenko.** Semenko called for throwing out old traditions, yet he shaped modern Ukrainian poetry himself: the city and play with form and language became part of it. At 21 he dared to challenge the whole literary tradition, then gathered rebellious artists around him again and again, from Fliamingo to Nova Generatsiia. Writing about the city and the new age in the spirit of Marinetti, he showed that Ukrainian poetry could speak the language of its own time. He was shot in 1937, but his Poems came out in 1985, and the rowdy Futurist is read again. Anyone looking for new forms for Ukrainian today walks a road he helped lay.
- **Mykhailo Dianov.** Mykhailo Dianov went to defend Ukraine as a volunteer back in 2015, and in Mariupol, with a badly wounded arm, he stayed with his comrades at Azovstal. His photos from before and after captivity became evidence to the world of what Russia does to prisoners of war. Once home, he gave all 23.4 million hryvnias raised for his treatment to treat wounded fellow soldiers, and promised to report on every payment. “My main goal is fewer amputations and more veterans with healthy limbs,” he said. His choice showed that for a soldier, what matters most is the comrades who still need help, and that people’s trust can be passed on.

</details>

Книжки Костомарова й сьогодні допомагають побачити в історії України не лише гетьманів, а й звичайних людей. |

<details><summary>English, batch 42</summary>

- **Mykhailo Drahomanov.** Dismissed from his university, Drahomanov did not go quiet but became Ukraine’s voice in Europe. In Geneva he published Hromada, the first Ukrainian political journal, and pamphlets that told Europeans how Ukrainians lived in the Russian Empire. He became the intellectual mentor of young Galicians, including [Ivan Franko](/en/people/ivan-franko/), and left more than two thousand works on history, folklore and literary studies. He pictured a state built on decentralization and self-governing communities, and summed up his outlook in a slogan: “Cosmopolitanism in the ideas and the ends, nationality in the ground and the forms.” Anyone who explains Ukraine to the world today follows his path.
- **Mykhailo Drapatyi.** Drapatyi is cited as part of a new generation of Ukrainian generals shaped by combat since 2014 rather than Soviet-era training. In 2014 he led his battalion out of an encirclement, saving more than 30 pieces of equipment, and in 2022 he took command of the grouping that liberated the right bank of the Kherson region. When a missile strike on a training ground killed 12 soldiers in 2025, he publicly took responsibility as commander and resigned. He has called wartime service “absolute responsibility”. His path from battalion commander to Commander-in-Chief shows how the war has reshaped the leadership of Ukraine’s armed forces.
- **Mykhailo Tkach.** Investigative journalism is one way a society keeps watch over those in power. Mykhailo Tkach started as a student at a local channel in Dnipro; on Schemes he put out dozens of investigations into the country’s most senior officials. During the full-scale war he looked into trade routes with Russia and officials who went on holiday abroad while travel was restricted. His work shows that even in wartime, questions to officials still matter. He received the [Georgiy Gongadze](/en/people/heorhii-honhadze/) Prize in 2026 not for a single story but for his career and loyalty to the values of journalism. Anyone who asks the powerful awkward questions helps keep a country honest.
- **Mykhailo Verbytskyi.** A song a village priest wrote for the guitar became the music of Ukraine’s state anthem. In the spring of 1863, in Mlyny, Mykhailo Verbytskyi set [Pavlo Chubynskyi](/en/people/pavlo-chubynskyi/)’s poem Shche ne vmerla Ukraina to music, first for voice and guitar, later for choir and orchestra, and on 15 January 1992 that melody became the anthem of an independent state. He wrote for the stage and for the church, set [Taras Shevchenko](/en/people/taras-shevchenko/)’s Zapovit for choir and composed Galicia’s anthem too, so he wrote the tunes of two anthems. Every time Ukrainians sing their anthem, it is his music they hear, and at his grave in Mlyny they traditionally sing it.
- **Mykola Kostomarov.** Born a serf, Kostomarov became a professor and spent his life arguing that Ukrainians are a people with a history of their own. For writing the programme of the Cyril and Methodius Brotherhood he spent a year in the Peter and Paul Fortress, and later he tracked down documents in 65 archives and libraries to write about Khmelnytskyi, the Ruin and Mazepa. His ideas about democracy and federation shaped not only historical scholarship but also the Ukrainian national movement, and [Mykhailo Hrushevskyi](/en/people/mykhailo-hrushevskyi/) called some of his essays “canonical”. Kostomarov’s books still help readers see in Ukraine’s history not only hetmans but ordinary people too.

</details>

Він доводив, що всі людські раси єдині й рівні, а «нижчих рас» не існує, і не лише писав про це: виступав проти работоргівлі, а за його порадою нідерландська влада взялася припиняти торгівлю людьми на Тернате і Тідоре. Після 1861 року він лікував хворих у Вишні, де збудував лікарню й аптеку; сьогодні його ім'я носить Вінницький національний медичний університет. |

А його «Євшан-зілля» - поема про те, як не втратити пам'ять про свій край. | **Чому це важливо сьогодні**<br>Вороний одним із перших відкрив українську поезію новим течіям. Його маніфест 1901 року закликав письменників шукати нові шляхи, а з альманаху «З-над хмар і з долин» почався український модернізм як програма; поруч із модерністами там друкувалися [Іван Франко](/uk/people/ivan-franko/) і [Леся Українка](/uk/people/lesya-ukrainka/). Він служив і сцені: грав у трупах Кропивницького й Саксаганського, 1917 року став одним із засновників національного театру й Центральної Ради, перекладав українською оперні лібрето, зокрема «Кармен» і «Ріґолетто». Тож кожен, хто сьогодні шукає для української культури нових форм, іде шляхом, який він прокладав. А його «Євшан-зілля» - поема про те, як не втратити пам'ять про свій край. |
| Назарій Гринцевич | **Як його пам'ятають**<br>8 липня 2025 року Назарію посмертно присвоїли звання Героя України. Для багатьох він став уособленням покоління, яке пішло захищати країну просто зі студентської лави. А його слова, записані навесні 2022 року в Маріуполі, - «Що б не було у вас, любіть маму, їжте кашу і любіть Україну» - досі повторюють як просте й щире напуття. | **Чому це важливо сьогодні**<br>Назарій Гринцевич пішов захищати країну просто зі студентської лави, і для багатьох став уособленням цього покоління. Три місяці він був бойовим медиком «Азову» на «Азовсталі», пережив полон, а після реабілітації повернувся до служби: разом із побратимами заснував підрозділ артилерійської розвідки «Контакт 12» і командував у ньому взводом. Сам він казав: «Я - звичайний солдат», а те, що захисники вийшли з «Азовсталі», пояснював не собою, а командиром [Денисом Прокопенком](/uk/people/denys-prokopenko/). Посмертно він став Героєм України, а його напуття з Маріуполя - «Що б не було у вас, любіть маму, їжте кашу і любіть Україну» - досі повторюють як просте й щире слово до кожного. |

<details><summary>English, batch 43</summary>

- **Mykola Mikhnovskyi.** When most of the national movement asked only for autonomy, Mikhnovskyi insisted on full independence from Russia, guarded by an army of Ukraine’s own. His pamphlet Independent Ukraine turned the idea of a free state into a political programme, and he later co-wrote a draft constitution for it. In 1917 he set about building one: he founded the Polubotok Ukrainian Military Club and helped form the Bohdan Khmelnytskyi Regiment. His ideas shaped the generation of the 1920s and 1930s, above all in Western Ukraine and in the emigration. A century after his death, the question of how to live next to Russia has again become a question of survival.
- **Mykola Miklukho-Maklai.** For about three years Miklukho-Maklai lived among the Papuans of New Guinea, winning their trust with patience, courage and a doctor’s skills. He argued that all human races are one and equal, and he acted on it: he fought the slave trade, and on his advice the Dutch moved to stop it on Ternate and Tidore. He left about 160 works and a marine biological station in Sydney, and his diary, Among the Savages of New Guinea, was translated into Ukrainian by [Borys Antonenko-Davydovych](/en/people/borys-antonenko-davydovych/). The coast where he lived is still called the Maclay Coast, and his idea that no people is higher or lower than another still sounds modern.
- **Mykola Pyrohov.** Painkilling during surgery, a plaster cast on a broken arm and sorting the wounded by urgency seem ordinary today, and Pyrohov was among those who made them part of medicine. His atlas of frozen sections showed surgeons where each organ lies. In Ukraine he also worked for education: he argued for teaching in the native language and for schooling open to all, helped open Sunday schools in Kyiv that taught in Ukrainian too, and on his initiative the Richelieu Lyceum in Odesa became a university. After 1861 he treated the sick at Vyshnia, where he built a hospital; today Vinnytsia National Medical University bears his name.
- **Mykola Voronyi.** Voronyi was one of the first to open Ukrainian poetry to new currents. His 1901 manifesto urged writers to seek new paths, and his almanac From Above the Clouds and From the Valleys is where Ukrainian modernism began as a programme; next to the modernists it printed [Ivan Franko](/en/people/ivan-franko/) and [Lesya Ukrainka](/en/people/lesya-ukrainka/). He served the stage too: he acted with Kropyvnytskyi’s and Saksahanskyi’s troupes, co-founded the national theatre and the Central Rada in 1917, and translated librettos such as Carmen and Rigoletto into Ukrainian. Anyone seeking new forms for Ukrainian culture walks a path he helped open. His Yevshan-zillia is a poem about not losing the memory of your land.
- **Nazarii Hryntsevych.** Nazarii Hryntsevych went to defend his country straight from the lecture hall, and for many he stands for that generation. For three months he was an Azov combat medic at Azovstal; he survived captivity, then went back to service, founding the Kontakt 12 artillery reconnaissance unit with his comrades and commanding a platoon in it. He called himself “an ordinary soldier” and credited the survival of Azovstal’s defenders not to himself but to their commander, [Denys Prokopenko](/en/people/denys-prokopenko/). He was made a Hero of Ukraine posthumously, and his words from Mariupol, “Whatever happens to you, love your mum, eat your porridge and love Ukraine”, are still repeated as simple, sincere advice.

</details>

<details><summary>English, batch 44</summary>

- **Nina Matviienko.** Nina Matviienko brought ancient ritual songs, carols, spring songs, Kupala and harvest songs, to modern listeners. Her voice inspired composers: Stankovych, Skoryk and Kyva wrote especially for her, and she showed that folk singing can stand alongside classical music. Then she proved it alive next to rock, singing with Okean Elzy, Tanok na Maidani Kongo and [Oleh Skrypka](/en/people/oleh-skrypka/), and performing with the La MaMa theater in New York. She explained herself why it matters: “When there are traditions, there is a nation, there is a people, there is a state.” Anyone who sings a carol today carries on what she kept alive for over half a century.
- **Oksana Baiul.** Oksana Baiul was the first to raise Ukraine’s flag to the top step of an Olympic podium, and the organizers had to hunt for a recording of the anthem. Her path shows you needn’t start out winning: at Soviet championships she never finished above tenth, yet a year after moving to Odesa she was world champion. In Lillehammer she was hurt in practice but still skated, added an unplanned jump combination near the end, and won by a tenth of a point. As of 2020 she was still Ukraine’s only Olympic champion in figure skating. Anyone who doubts themselves can remember how a 16-year-old from Dnipro didn’t give up.
- **Oksana Rubaniak.** Oksana Rubaniak went to defend Ukraine on the first day of the full-scale invasion, at 19. Her comrades doubted she could handle a machine gun, yet for a year and a half she was the only woman in her platoon; wounded, she returned to service and later recruited and trained her own drone platoon. She wrote her poems at the front: “Towards Death” has been called a trench diary in verse, and she dedicated “The Road of Life” to fallen comrades. A young soldier from the Carpathians, she tells the story of the war in the first person and keeps the memory of those who stood beside her.
- **Oleh Mikats.** Oleh Mikats was the first Ukrainian general who had never served in the Soviet army: his whole career was in the armed forces of independent Ukraine. In 2014 his brigade took Pisky and Pervomaiske, giving Ukrainian forces control of the road between Donetsk and Dnipropetrovsk, and from September he led the defense of Donetsk airport, whose defenders became known as the “cyborgs.” Mikats is convinced that the airport’s defense mattered for the whole war: “The airport tied down the enemy’s forces.” After the airport he took over the Desna Training Center, and in 2021 Operational Command East. His example shows the kind of commander independent Ukraine’s army has raised.
- **Oleh Sentsov.** Sentsov’s case drew the world’s attention to Ukrainians persecuted by Russia for resisting the occupation of Crimea. In the penal colony he went 145 days without food, not for himself but to demand freedom for all Ukrainian political prisoners in Russia. At his trial he said it plainly: “A court of occupiers cannot be fair by definition.” Once free, he went back to filmmaking; after the full-scale invasion he joined the territorial defence, fought for Kyiv and later served in the 47th Brigade. His path from debut director to political prisoner, Sakharov Prize laureate and soldier has become, for many, an example of how an artist answers a war.

</details>

Його твори зберігаються в багатьох музеях світу, зокрема в Києві та Львові. | **Чому це важливо сьогодні**<br>Архипенко змінив саме уявлення про скульптуру: він показав, що вона «може починатися там, де простір охоплений матеріалом», і зробив порожнечу частиною форми. Його кубістичні фігури, поєднання металу, скла, дерева й пластмаси та «скульптомалярство» вплинули на модерне мистецтво Європи та Америки, а в Берліні й США він відкривав мистецькі школи. Здобувши визнання далеко від дому, він не відірвався від свого: створив портрети [Шевченка](/uk/people/taras-shevchenko/) та [Франка](/uk/people/ivan-franko/), а 1933 року виставив 48 робіт в українському павільйоні в Чикаго. Тож, побачивши в музеї скульптуру з наскрізним отвором, згадайте киянина, який одним із перших так працював. |
| Олександр Будько | **Чому це важливо сьогодні**<br>Через війну десятки тисяч українців утратили руки чи ноги. Олександр показує їм і всім іншим, що поранення не скасовує ні спорту, ні творчості, ні звичайного життя. А ще він обстоює безбар'єрну Україну, де людям з інвалідністю жити не важче, ніж іншим. | **Чому це важливо сьогодні**<br>Через війну десятки тисяч українців утратили руки чи ноги. Олександр показує їм і всім іншим, що поранення не скасовує ні спорту, ні творчості, ні звичайного життя: приблизно за три місяці після поранення він став на протези, а за рік виграв медаль Ігор Нескорених. Він приходить до поранених у шпиталі й підказує, як обрати протез чи отримати допомогу від держави, а у відео й книжці «Історія впертого чоловіка» з оптимізмом розповідає про життя на протезах. І нагадує, що «інклюзивність - це не про жалість, це про рівні права та комфорт для кожного». Тож кожен, хто робить свою вулицю, школу чи місто доступнішими, продовжує його справу. |
| Олександр Ройтбурд | **Чому це важливо сьогодні**<br>Ройтбурд довів, що старий музей може стати місцем, куди хочеться прийти. А його картини й відео досі показують, яким сміливим було українське мистецтво після здобуття незалежності. | **Чому це важливо сьогодні**<br>Ройтбурд довів, що старий музей може стати місцем, куди хочеться прийти. Він хотів перетворити Одеський художній музей на «міський форум» - і там з'явилися концерти, кінопокази, табори для дітей і дні безкоштовного входу, а понад сотня підприємців, митців і громадських діячів об'єдналися, щоб купувати для музею нові роботи. Коли його звільнили, одесити вийшли на мітинги на його підтримку, а Верховний Суд визнав звільнення незаконним. Ще в 1990-х він допомагав будувати в Одесі сучасне мистецтво, а його відео потрапило до MoMA. Його картини й відео досі показують, яким сміливим було українське мистецтво після здобуття незалежності. |
| Олександр Шемет | **Чому це важливо**<br>«Свобода дається дорого», - казав Шемет про побратимів, які не повертаються із завдань. Гелікоптери були єдиною ниткою між оточеним заводом і рештою України, і льотчики летіли туди, знаючи, що повернуться не всі. | **Чому це важливо сьогодні**<br>Гелікоптери були єдиною ниткою між оточеним «Азовсталем» і рештою України, і льотчики летіли туди, знаючи, що повернуться не всі. Олександр Шемет полетів: доправив оборонцям вантаж і повернув додому машину й вісьмох тяжкопоранених. Це був останній успішний авіапрорив до заводу. «Свобода дається дорого», - казав він про побратимів, які не повертаються із завдань. Його служба тривала з 2015 року: АТО на сході, оборона Києва, а згодом і перехоплення «шахедів». Він показав, що навіть до оточених своїх можна дістатися, коли хтось готовий узяти на себе цей ризик. |

<details><summary>English, batch 45</summary>

- **Oleh Skrypka.** Skrypka is called a legend of Ukrainian ethno-rock: in his songs and at Kraina Mrii, folk tradition sounds modern. His path shows that you can find your Ukrainian self: born in Tajikistan and raised in Russia, he first felt Ukrainian in France, then came back and brought folk tradition onto the rock stage. “Vesna” marked the band’s return to the Ukrainian stage, and Kraina Mrii invites visitors not just to listen to tradition but to join in. After the full-scale invasion began, his family stayed in Kyiv, and in 2024 he turned his jubilee concert into a collection “for Victory.”
- **Alexander Archipenko.** Archipenko changed the idea of sculpture: he showed that it “may begin where space is encircled by the material,” and made empty space part of the form. His Cubist figures, mix of metal, glass, wood and plastic, and “sculpto-paintings” influenced modern art in Europe and America, and he opened art schools in Berlin and the United States. Famous far from home, he kept his ties: he made portraits of [Shevchenko](/en/people/taras-shevchenko/) and [Franko](/en/people/ivan-franko/), and in 1933 showed 48 works in the Ukrainian pavilion in Chicago. So when you see a sculpture with a hole in it, think of the Kyiv-born artist who was one of the first to work that way.
- **Oleksandr Budko.** The war has left tens of thousands of Ukrainians without an arm or a leg. Oleksandr shows them, and everyone else, that a wound doesn’t cancel sport, creative work or ordinary life: three months after his wound he stood on prosthetic legs, and a year later won an Invictus Games medal. He visits the wounded in hospital to advise on prostheses and state help, and his videos and book, The Story of a Stubborn Man, cheerfully show life on prostheses. “Inclusivity is not about pity,” he says, “it is about equal rights and comfort for everyone.” Anyone making their street or school easier to get around carries on his work.
- **Oleksandr Roitburd.** Roitburd showed that an old museum can become a place people want to visit. He set out to turn the Odesa Art Museum into a “city forum,” and it filled with concerts, film screenings, children’s camps and free-admission days, while more than a hundred businesspeople, artists and public figures joined forces to buy it new works. When he was dismissed, people in Odesa rallied for him, and the Supreme Court ruled the dismissal unlawful. Back in the 1990s he had helped build contemporary art in Odesa, and his video ended up at MoMA. His paintings and videos still show how bold Ukrainian art was after independence.
- **Oleksandr Shemet.** The helicopters were the only thread between the besieged steelworks and the rest of Ukraine, and the pilots flew there knowing that not all of them would return. Oleksandr Shemet went: he delivered the cargo to the defenders and brought the aircraft and eight badly wounded men home. It was the last successful air run to Azovstal. “Freedom comes at a high price,” he said of comrades who don’t come back from missions. His service ran from 2015: the anti-terrorist operation, the defence of Kyiv, and later intercepting Shahed drones. He showed that even encircled comrades can be reached when someone is willing to take on the risk.

</details>

<details><summary>English, batch 46</summary>

- **Oleksandr Smakula.** The thin layer that tames glare moved from optical instruments to everyday glasses. It was developed by Oleksandr Smakula, a boy from Dobrovody who made it to the labs of Carl Zeiss and MIT. At MIT he spent over 30 years as a professor and ran the crystal physics lab, and physics has a “Smakula formula.” Far from home, he kept his ties: a full member of the Shevchenko Scientific Society and honorary president of the Ukrainian Engineers’ Society of America. So the next time an optician offers you anti-reflective lenses, think of the physicist from a village near Ternopil who stood at the start of that idea.
- **Oleksandr Trepak.** The defense of Donetsk airport became one of the symbols of the war that began in 2014. The word “cyborg” has since come to mean a defender who cannot be broken, and Oleksandr Trepak is one of the people it started with. Under him the defenders beat back every assault and were not cut off from food and ammunition. Near Artemivsk he had held a checkpoint for three hours so others could get a wounded commander out, then, wounded himself, refused evacuation. He credits his soldiers: “I am nothing without my guys.” He later commanded his own regiment and, from 2024, the Special Operations Forces.
- **Oleksandr Usyk.** Usyk became undisputed champion in two weight classes and the first to win that title three times. From Olympic gold in London he went on to hold the belts of all the major boxing organizations: in 2018 he became the first Ukrainian undisputed world champion, then proved he could beat the biggest boxers at heavyweight too. He showed that a boxer from Ukraine can hold every major belt at once, and do it again. And in February 2022, at the peak of his career, he came home to defend Kyiv and put a rematch on hold: “It is my duty to fight, to defend my home, my family.”
- **Oleksandra Oliynykova.** Oliynykova could have stayed a Croatian player, but in 2021, before the full-scale invasion, she chose Ukraine. She kept training in Kyiv even when her flat had no electricity or heat, won three WTA 125 titles in 2025 and climbed to No. 44 in the world in 2026. She uses every big tournament to keep the world from getting used to the war: “Some players are tired to speak about this now. I’m not.” And she turns her fame into help, raising money for the unit her father fights in and co-founding the drones4ua.org drive for drones. She shows that an athlete can be her country’s voice.
- **Oleksii Anulia.** Oleksii tells the world about captivity because his voice speaks for the Ukrainians still held in Russian prisons. After captivity and dozens of hospital stays, he took on a new cause: he campaigns for the release of other prisoners, supports families of the missing and testifies to war crimes, in a UN hall in Geneva and in his book “Jingle bellz”. His case is registered with the International Criminal Court in The Hague. Dignity, he says, is when “you can give a part of yourself for the good of another.” Oleksii Anulia’s story is a reminder that every prisoner must be spoken about until they are brought home.

</details>
